Title: Quantum Satake in Type A: The General and Generic Case

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.23253v1

Published Time: Tue, 22 Sep 2026 01:31:38 GMT

Number of Pages: 103

Markdown Content:
QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE                   

> BEN ELIAS AND KOPPARA PHILIP THOMAS
> Abstract. In work of the first author, the geometric Satake equivalence was (non-rigorously) reinterpreted as an equivalence between two algebraically-defined 2-categories, one built from representations of a Lie algebra, and one built using singular Soergel bimodules. It was then explained how to q-deform this equivalence in type A, replacing the special linear Lie algebra with its quantum group, and using singular Soergel bimodules for a deformed reflection representation. Both the algebraic reformulation and its q-deformation were only proven in types A1and A2. In this paper, we prove the result in type An−1for n≥4, while working generically: more precisely, we work over a field of characteristic zero, where qis not a root of unity, and having adjoined an n-th root of q. Along the way we generalize certain results in the literature (e.g. the Soergel-Williamson categorification theorem, the Soergel conjecture for spherical elements, various symmetries) to the deformed reflection representation.

1. Introduction 

1.1. Geometric Satake and numerical coincidences. In [Eli17], the geometric Satake equivalence [Lus83, Gin95, MV07] was reinterpreted algebraically. This reinterpretation makes sense in any type, but was only made explicit in type A, where we now focus our attention. On one side of the equivalence is a 2-category built from the Q-linear representation theory of sl n, which we call Rep Ω sl n. The objects of this 2-category form a group Ω ∼= Z/n Z, which is isomorphic to the quotient of the weight lattice by the root lattice; the 1-morphisms are repre-sentations whose weights live in the appropriate coset within Ω, and 1-morphism composition is tensor product of representations. Loosely speaking, Rep Ω sl n is just the monoidal category Rep sl n with additional bookkeeping. On the other side of the equivalence is a 2-category of singular Soergel bimodules [Wil11] in type ˜An−1, which can be thought of as an algebraic replacement for equivariant perverse sheaves on the affine Grassmannian. Crucial to note is that the vertices of the affine Dynkin diagram ˜An−1 are in bijection with Ω, and these vertices also parametrize copies of the finite Weyl group Sn inside the affine Weyl group Waff as (standard) parabolic subgroups. These finite parabolic subgroups are objects in the 2-category of singular Soergel bimodules SSBim. The 1-morphisms are particular bimodules over certain polynomial rings. The geometric Satake equivalence becomes a (conjectural) Q-linear 2-functor GS from Rep Ω sl n to SSBim. Note that singular Soergel bimodules are a graded category whereas representations are not, so one cannot expect GS to be an equivalence. Instead it is a degree zero equivalence : it is fully faithful onto the space of degree zero 2-morphisms, and there are no 2-morphisms of negative degree between the 1-morphisms in its image. We call this the 

Soergelified (geometric) Satake equivalence . We refer to it as conjectural for two reasons. First, the reformulation from traditional geometric Satake to Soergelified Satake was sketched but not made rigorous in [Eli17]. Second, even were it made rigorous, geometric Satake would imply the existence of a 2-functor GS, but it would not be obvious how to make this 2-functor explicit on the level of 2-morphisms.   

> 1arXiv:2609.23253v1 [math.RT] 19 Sep 2026 2QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE

In [Eli17] an explicit (potential) construction of the 2-functor GS is given for all n ≥ 2, and it is proven correct for n = 2 , 3. The construction uses webs, which are a generators-and-relations description of the monoidal category Rep sl n. For n = 2 webs form the well-known Temperley-Lieb category; for n = 3 they were constructed by Kuperberg [Kup96]; for 

n > 3 they were constructed by Cautis-Kamnitzer-Morrison [CKM14] based on earlier work of Morrison. Concretely, [Eli17] explains where the generating webs should go under GS, but only checks the relations for n = 2 , 3. In this paper we check the relations for n > 3. Amongst the relations defining the web category, various signed binomial coefficients appear. For example, the value of a 1-labeled circle in the web category is ( −1) n−1(n

> 1

) = ( −1) n−1n,which is the categorical dimension of the standard representation of sl n. Meanwhile, the construction of singular Soergel bimodules takes as input the reflection representation V of 

Waff , equipped with a choice of simple roots and coroots. The technical heart of Soergelified Satake is the realization that these binomial coefficients are also coded within V . The simplest example of this phenomenon is the fact that −2, the value of the Temperley-Lieb circle, agrees with the off-diagonal entry in the Cartan matrix for ˜A1.The representation V is spanned by the simple roots {αi}i∈Ω, and the action of Waff is governed by the affine Cartan matrix. Let R = Raff be the polynomial ring whose linear terms are V . For each simple reflection si, i ∈ Ω, one defines Demazure operators ∂i : R → R,

∂i(f ) = f − si(f )

More generally, one can realize binomial coefficients by applying certain sequences of Demazure operators to certain products of positive roots. For example, when n = 4, one has 

∂1∂2∂3 (α0 · s1(α0) · s2s1(α0)) = −4 = ( −1) 4−1

. (1.3) To our knowledge, these observations and related numerical coincidences have not yet appeared in the literature. The computations (1.2) and (1.3) arise when checking that the circle relations of the web category continue to hold in SSBim after applying GS. The product of roots appears via a coproduct for a Frobenius extension between subrings of R, and the sequence of Demazure operators is the trace map for a Frobenius extension between different subrings of R. To check the remaining web relations requires more computations of a similar flavor: applying Demazure operators to tensors (of polynomials) built from Frobenius coproducts. These computations can be quite thorny. Once one has constructed the functor GS, one can prove the rest of the Soergelified Satake equivalence from scratch (i.e. without going through geometric Satake). It remains to show that GS is a degree zero equivalence, and here is a brief sketch. Williamson’s Hom formula (Theorem 3.21) shows that the graded dimension of 2-morphism spaces between singular So-ergel bimodules is determined by a certain pairing on its Grothendieck group. The seminal work of Lusztig [Lus83] determines the images of (characters of) irreducible representations under the Satake isomorphism (i.e. the decategorification of the geometric Satake equivalence). These results combine to show that the dimensions of the 2-morphism spaces are appropriate QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 3

for GS to be a degree zero equivalence. In particular, if GS is faithful then it is also full (to degree zero) by a dimension count. If we define our categories to be Q-linear, then we can continue as follows. The semisimple 2-category Rep Ω sl n has no nontrivial monoidal ideals (see Theorem 5.25 for a proof, but this is an adaptation of a well-known argument), so the kernel of GS is either zero or everything. The latter is absurd, since identity maps go to identity maps. Hence GS is faithful. This proof will not work integrally or over fields of finite characteristic, where nontrivial monoidal ideals exist; we will have more to say about this problem later. 1.2. The quantum deformation. One side of the Satake equivalence has a well-known q-deformation: the representation theory of the quantum group Uq(sl n). These can also be described with webs, and quantum binomial coefficients replace ordinary binomial coefficients. Interestingly, [Eli17] introduced a q-deformed Cartan matrix of type ˜An−1, leading to a defor-mations Vq and Rq, and to a deformation of singular Soergel bimodules which currently has no analogue in geometry. The quantum (Soergelified) Satake equivalence is the corresponding (conjectural) C(q)-linear degree zero equivalence GS q from Rep Ω Uq(sl n) to q-deformed singular Soergel bimodules. Here is the deformed Cartan matrix in type ˜A3.



2 −1 0 −q−1

−1 2 −1 00 −1 2 −q

−q 0 −q−1 2

 (1.4) Repeating the computation of (1.2) for this Cartan matrix, one obtains 

∂1∂2∂3 (α0 · s1(α0) · s2s1(α0)) = −[4] q . (1.5) Once again, [Eli17] explicitly constructed GS q for n = 2 , 3 and proved it was well-defined. The construction of GS q for n ≥ 4 as a deformation of GS entails a choice of invertible scalar for each generating web, such that the web relations hold after applying GS q. In this paper we provide those scalars and check the relations, thus constructing GS q for n ≥ 4. We also prove that this 2-functor is a degree zero equivalence over Q(q), finishing the work begun in [Eli17]. The details can be found in Theorem 5.31. More accurately, we work over an extension of 

Q(q), see §1.4. The technical heart of our proof is a number of thorny computations with Demazure opera-tors acting on Rq. This requires even more care than one might expect, because the q-deformed Demazure operators no longer satisfy the braid relations, so one must keep careful track of Frobenius structures and transformations between reduced expressions in addition to the other algebraic work. Our proof that GS q is a degree zero equivalence over Q(q) follows the same outline as for GS in the previous section. However, Williamson did not prove his Hom formula in this level of generality. The representation Vq is not balanced (see §3.2), a technical condition without which one cannot pick out positive versus negative roots consistently. The Hom formula is one of several basic facts about Soergel bimodules which had not been proven for the q-deformation, a situation we rectify in this paper. This also allows us to use certain shortcuts: knowing the dimensions of morphism spaces allows us to bypass certain computations. Since our goal is to establish the quantum geometric Satake equivalence rigorously, and since the literature has a tendency to wave its hands at some technicalities, we will be concrete and fill in many details. So that later work can more easily establish the integral or finite characteristic versions of (quantum) Soergel Satake without redoing fundamental work, we 4 QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 

prove our results over general base rings when possible. For example, we introduce reflection faithfulness (another technical condition) over general base rings, and show that the q-deformed reflection representation is reflection faithful. Along similar lines, the literature on geometric Satake tends to use the extended affine Weyl group, whereas we prefer using different parabolic subgroups in the affine Weyl group. There are some frustrations in matching the choices of convention (coming from right actions vs. left actions 1, positive vs. negative Iwahori subgroups, etcetera), so we redevelop some of the combinatorics from scratch. We provide a description of the combinatorial correspondence between dominant weights and double cosets in a fully explicit fashion, focusing on particular representatives of these cosets which are best adapted to the concept of a “singular reduced expression” as introduced in [Wil08, EK23]. Another goal of this paper was originally to rigorously establish the relationship between Soergel Satake and geometric Satake (in the non-quantum setting). This is work in progress and should hopefully appear in an appendix to a future version of this paper. The relationship is sketched in [Eli17, §6] but no attempt is made at a rigorous proof there. The reader who is curious about the relationship is encouraged to read the introduction of [Eli17], which instructs the geometrically-minded reader on how to skip most of the paper and efficiently reach §6. 1.3. Remarks on integral forms. In [Eli17] it was promised that the general case of quantum Soergel Satake would be proven in a sequel. The twelve year time lapse between [Eli17] and its sequel (the present paper) deserves some explanation. In fact, what [Eli17] proposed is a stronger result about the integral forms of these categories, which we discuss now. We do not prove this stronger result. 

Remark 1.1. The first author apologizes for their youthful optimism; the intervening years have cured both these flaws, youthfulness and optimism. At the time of [Eli17] the basic computations like (1.5) had been done (and (1.3) for n = 4), but not the nastier ones. By presenting a category by generators and relations, one makes it straightforward to define functors therefrom. Another aspect of a presentation is that it defines an integral form of the category. The category one is presenting may originally be Q-linear (or Q(q)-linear), but if all the coefficients in all the relations live in a subring A (like Z or Z[q, q −1]), then one can define an A-linear category by generators and relations instead. We think of an integral form as being “correct” if morphism spaces are free over A, and specialize in an interesting way (e.g. to finite characteristic, or setting q to be a root of unity). It was proven in [Eli15] that Cautis-Kamnitzer-Morrison’s webs are the correct integral form for Rep Ω sl n, in that they specialize to describe the category of tilting modules over any field. Meanwhile, it was already known by the authors of [CKM14] that many of their relations were redundant when working non-integrally, over Q(q). For example, a streamlined presenta-tion can be found in work of Bigelow [Big25] (which also fixes a sign error in [CKM14]), and we reiterate that Bigelow’s presentation is knowingly the “incorrect” integral form. Our construction of GS and GS q factors through a diagrammatic description of SSBim which we denote D. While an integral form for ordinary Soergel bimodules was found in the early 2010s by Elias, Khovanov, and Williamson [EK10, Eli16, EW16], constructing an integral form for singular Soergel bimodules remains a long-standing programme (one which the youthful optimist expected to have been concluded by now). A diagrammatic 2-category was constructed in [EWS16], called D(Γ) in the body of the paper, with a 2-functor to SSBim which is full (proven 2 in [EKLP24]) but known not to be faithful; there are not enough relations in   

> 1The commutativity of the spherical Hecke algebra has obscured the effects of some conventional issues.
> 2in the case when the realization is balanced. QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 5

[EWS16]. Let D be the quotient of D(Γ) by the kernel, so that D maps fully faithfully to SSBim. Some of the relations in this kernel are known from the literature (e.g. from [Eli16, EL17]), but it is expected that more relations are required, especially to get the “correct” integral form. Thus D is not very explicit in its current state, but is nonetheless a useful language to encode morphisms in SSBim. One of the “shortcomings” of this paper is that we do not prove our 2-functor GS q is well-defined using only known relations in D, but resort to calculations in SSBim. We cannot be certain, but feel there are not enough known relations to do the job, so we await an improvement in the available diagrammatic technology. Since the state of the art constrains us to work with 

SSBim and not to work with an integral form thereof, we avail ourselves of certain tricks. We use the Soergel-Williamson Hom formula (which specifies the dimension of certain morphism spaces) to simplify some computations. We avail ourselves of Bigelow’s presentation. We have been referring to the (not-explicitly-defined) 2-category D as an integral form for 

SSBim, but this is abuse of terminology. It should be an integral form for the Hecke 2-category ,which is supposed to categorify the Hecke algebroid in such a way that the Soergel-Williamson Hom formula holds. It is known that singular Soergel bimodules are not well-behaved in some settings (e.g. finite characteristic in affine type) so they are only a good model for the Hecke 2-category generically, not for its integral form. Abe [Abe25] has provided an algebraic model for the Hecke 2-category, though not a presentation. When the programme to present the Hecke 2-category is complete, there should be a version of D defined over Z[q, q −1], with a presentation, such that morphism spaces are free over 

Z[q, q −1] of the expected size. Showing freeness given a presentation is very difficult work! What it would take to upgrade our 2-functor GS q to a 2-functor from Cautis-Kamnitzer-Morrison’s presentation to the integral form of D? What remains is less difficult, though there are some subtleties. We have defined the 2-functor on objects and morphisms already, and need only check the relations. Most of the relations from [CKM14] have the form: take a diagram X, and rewrite it as a linear combination of elements in a basis B for this morphism space. Consider the collection of diagrams GS q(B) in D. This collection is linearly independent, which can be proven after base change to Q(q) where it follows from our results here. If GS( X) could be rewritten as a linear combination of GS q(B), then the coefficients must be as desired, again provable after base change to Q(q). However, it is not obvious that GS q(B) is a basis, even though it has the desired size; it might only span a Z[q, q −1]-sublattice. What remains is not a computational problem, but an abstract question: is GS q full? Major advances in the aforementioned programme were recently made by the first author together with Ko, Libedinsky, and Patimo [EK23, EKLP26b, EKLP23, EKLP26a, EKLP24]. In particular, in [EKLP24] a basis for 2-morphisms in SSBim is constructed combinatorially as the image of certain diagrams in D(Γ), called the double leaves basis 3. This is expected to be a basis for the correct integral form of D, once that integral form is established. Meanwhile, a combinatorial basis for webs was constructed in [Eli15], the double ladders basis . One way to prove the fullness of GS is to prove the following. 

Conjecture 1.2. Under GS (or GS q), double ladders are sent to double leaves (up to invertible scalar in Z[q, q −1]).    

> 3It is proven in [EKLP24] that the double leaves maps become a basis after applying the 2-functor to SSBim, but this is done under the balanced assumption. More work would be needed to adapt their arguments to the
> q-deformation. 6QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE

Double ladders are built from elementary light ladders, which live in one-dimensional mor-phism spaces modulo lower terms. That the image of elementary light ladders agrees with a (non-elementary) light leaf modulo lower terms is thus not surprising, but in the examples we have computed they agree on the nose. At first glance this conjecture seems straightforward, but it is surprisingly complex. We illustrate with an example, and though we have not yet introduced the diagrammatic categories in question, we expect the reader will get the point. Complete details on this example are found in Subsection 7.4, so pardon the incompleteness here. A relatively simple form of elementary light ladder is exemplified by the following web. We wish to note a change of variables that is used throughout the paper. The q-deformed Cartan matrix for ˜An from (1.4) has the nice feature that it contains the usual Cartan matrix for An inside, but it admits very little symmetry! Each parabolic subgroup of ˜An behaves a little bit differently. In the body of this paper, we do most computations for a different deformation of the usual ˜An Cartan matrix over a different formal variable ζ, exemplified here for n = 3. 



2 −ζ 0 −ζ−1

−ζ−1 2 −ζ 00 −ζ−1 2 −ζ

−ζ 0 −ζ−1 2

 (1.8) This Cartan matrix was introduced in [EJY24]. It leads to deformations Vζ and Rζ . We will also define a 2-functor GS ζ from Rep Ω sl n to SSBim as defined over Rζ .When ζn = q−2, there is an isomorphism Vq ∼= Vζ which intertwines the root data up to scalar. This change of variables requires an extension of Q(q). The extension Q(q 1 

> n

) will suffice, and we use this for the introduction, see Subsection 4.5.1 for more details. The advantage of the ζ-deformation is its obvious rotational symmetry (i.e. rotation of the Dynkin diagram, the action of Ω; not rotation of diagrams), a symmetry we denote σ in QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 7

this paper, and a symmetry shared by colored webs. Diagrammatically, σ will just change various labels (depicted as colors) in each diagram, without changing the underlying diagrams or rescaling them. The 2-functor GS ζ will preserve σ, making it easier to check relations. 

Remark 1.3. One could translate the symmetry σ through the isomorphism with Vq to obtain a symmetry on Vq and on categories built from Vq. However, this symmetry would not just relabel things but would also multiply them by annoying scalars. The disadvantage of the ζ deformation is that it obscures the quantum parameter q which is most common in the literature. Knowing the isomorphism Vq ∼= Vζ is not quite enough to easily convert from one parameter to another. The most natural choice of Frobenius extension structures between Rq and its subrings (all of which can be defined over Q(q) without needing an extension) differs from the most natural choice for Rζ . The very meaning of the diagrams we use (in D) depends on this choice of Frobenius extension structure. We distinguish the two diagrammatic categories as Dζ and Dq. In Subsection 7.2 we explicitly describe the way in which one converts between these two conventions. One might also hope to define GS q over Q(q) rather than its extension Q(q 1 

> n

), and this is possible, but we do not attempt to make it explicit, because of the following obnoxious issue. As noted earlier, diagrams in the web category are sent to scalar multiples of diagrams in D.We offer a particular preferred choice of these scalars in Theorem 5.18. What precisely these scalars are is not intrinsic to geometric Satake! One could obtain a new 2-functor GS ′ with different scalars by precomposing with an object-fixing autoequivalence of webs, one which rescales the generating morphisms. However, certain relationships between these scalars are required for the 2-functor to be well-defined, see (5.25). When we define GS ζ in Theorem 5.14, we assume implicitly that these scalars will be preserved by σ. Abandoning this assumption will lead to more bookkeeping. If we naively try to define GS q by using our preferred choice of scalars and pass through the equivalence between Dζ and Dq, the resulting scalars will live in Q(q 1 

> n

) and not the subring 

Q(q). One must choose scalars which are not preserved by σ in order to obtain a 2-functor defined over Q(q) (one must treat each parabolic subgroup a little differently). This is what was done for n = 3 in [Eli17]. Currently, the extra bookkeeping does not seem to be worth the effort, though we may spell this out in a future version of this paper. 1.5. More symmetries. Web categories have three “natural” symmetries we focus on: ro-tation of diagrams by 180 degrees, the horizontal flip, and the vertical flip. The 2-category 

D(Γ) from [EWS16] also has these symmetries 4. Note that the composition of a horizontal and a vertical flip is the rotation by 180 degrees. The 2-functor GS q appears to preserve these symmetries; a flipped web is sent to a flipped diagram (up to sign). However, as noted in §1.3, we are only able to construct a 2-functor to singular Soergel bimodules SSBim; not to D(Γ), nor to an explicit quotient thereof which a priori possesses the same symmetries. The bimodule 2-category SBS Bim also has three symmetries in the literature: adjunction, swapping the left and right actions, and (Poincar´ e) duality (in the same order as the corre-sponding diagrammatic symmetries above). Duality is the most complex of these, an exposition for which can be found in [BBE26, Section 3.4]. For an ( RI , R J )-bimodule B, its right dual (ignoring grading shifts) would be Hom (−,R I )(B, R I ), where this represents the space of mor-phisms as a right RI -module. Any singular Bott-Samelson bimodule is isomorphic to its right    

> 4In D(Γ), each strand in a diagram has an orientation, but this orientation is cosmetic, a convention to help one keep track of a labeling on regions. After flipping a diagram, convention dictates that one will also need to invert orientations. 8QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE

(or left) dual, and that isomorphism is defined by fixing a particular invariant bilinear form on the underlying bimodule. Crucially, these invariant bilinear forms can be defined using same Frobenius extension structures which are used to define adjunction. It is for this rea-son that “rotation” agrees with the composition of the “horizontal” and the “vertical” flip in the category of bimodules. This connection between bilinear forms and Frobenius structures is sometimes explicit and sometimes implicit in the literature, but what is seemingly absent from the literature is the statement that the 2-functor Φ from diagrams D(Γ) to bimodules intertwines these three symmetries, especially in the unbalanced case. We clarify the situ-ation in Subsection 7.3, and state the compatibility between GS q and these symmetries in Theorem 5.20. 1.6. Acknowledgments. Definition 2.1. Let ( W, S ) be a Coxeter system. A subset I ⊂ S is said to be finitary if the parabolic subgroup WI ⊂ W is finite. In this case we use wI to denote the longest element of 

WI , and set ℓ(I) := ℓ(wI ).

Definition 2.2. A subset I ⊂ S is maximal finitary if it is finitary, and maximal in the poset of finitary subsets of S. If I and J are maximal, then a double coset p ∈ WI \W/W J is called a maximal double coset 5.

Remark 2.3. When W is finite, there is a unique maximal finitary subset of S, namely S

itself, and a unique maximal double coset in W \W/W . It is for infinite Coxeter groups where maximal double cosets become interesting. The following construction is well-known, see [BB05, Chapter 8.3]. 

Theorem 2.4. Let SZ denote the permutations of Z. Fix n ≥ 2. Consider the subset of SZ

w(k + n) = w(k) + n for all k ∈ Z, (2.1)

As usual, we identify S with Ω = Z/n Z and consider indices (for simple reflections) modulo n.Viewing an element w ∈ W as living in SZ, the usual Coxeter length function can be described by 

ℓ(w) = # {(i, j ) ∈ Z × Z | i < j, w (i) > w (j)}/ ∼, (2.3)

of w, though abusing notation, we sometimes call the pair (i, j ) itself an inversion.

The goal of this section is to understand the bijection(s) between maximal double cosets in W and dominant weights which underlies geometric Satake, and to prove some statements about reduced expressions for these cosets, while making things as transparent as possible in the language of SZ.Throughout this chapter we fix n ≥ 2, and let ( W, S ) be the affine Weyl group as above. For a, b ∈ Ω we use notation like ̂ ab to denote the finitary subset S \ { sa, s b}.

Remark 2.5. Note that most approaches to geometric Satake in the literature use double cosets for the finite Weyl group within the extended affine Weyl group, rather than the collec-tion of all maximal double cosets in the affine Weyl group, and the bijection between dominant weights and cosets is usually phrased in that language. A dictionary between these approaches is provided in Subsection 7.1. 2.1. Distinguished representatives of cosets. We recall some standard facts about mini-mal and maximal representatives in cosets. 

Lemma 2.6. An element w ∈ W is minimal (in the Bruhat order) within its coset wW ̂ 0 if and only if 

w(1) < w (2) < . . . < w (n). (2.4) 

Equivalently, w has no inversions with a representative (i, j ) satisfying 1 ≤ i < j ≤ n.Similarly, w is maximal in wW ̂ 0 if w(1) > w (2) > . . . > w (n), and (i, j ) is an inversion for all 1 ≤ i < j ≤ n.Similarly, for all k ∈ Z, w is minimal in wW ̂ k if 

w(k + 1) < w (k + 2) < . . . < w (k + n),

and maximal if the order is reversed. An element w is minimal (resp. maximal) in Ŵ 0w if and only if w−1 is minimal (resp. maximal) in w−1Ŵ 0.Proof. This is straightforward, and left to the reader. 

Corollary 2.10. Fix k ∈ Z. Double cosets p ∈ Ŵ k\W/W ̂ 0 are in bijection with sequences 

(c1, . . . , c n) of integers satisfying (2.10) .Proof. By the previous lemma, a double coset p has a unique representative w giving rise to the integers ci. Conversely, given integers ci satisfying (2.10), let w(i) = k + i + cin, and extend this periodically to obtain a permutation of Z. Clearly (2.2) is satisfied so w ∈ W , and (2.9) is satisfied so w is the unique representative in its double coset. 

This corollary and lemma did not assume that 0 ≤ k < n , but applies for all k ∈ Z. Since 

Ŵ k = Ŵ k−n, this gives multiple different ways of encoding a double coset p in a sequence (c1, . . . , c n). In particular, shifting k 7 → k − n will shift ci 7 → ci + 1, thus appropriately shifting ∑ ci 7 → ∑ ci + n so that (2.10) still holds. By enforcing that 0 ≤ k < n , one obtains a unique sequence ( ci) associated to each maximal double coset. We summarize as follows.

2.4. Composition. Theorem 2.26 argues that the length of distinguished coset representatives is additive. In fact, this is because of a much stronger statement, that distinguished coset representatives compose as dominant weights add, and that this composition is reduced (the lengths add). However, when composing coset representatives, as for composing morphisms in a category, we should be careful that the sources and targets match appropriately. Given a coset p ∈ WI \W/W J and a coset q ∈ WJ \W/W K , we can multiply their distinguished representatives and hope to get a distinguished representative for a coset p ⋆ q in WI \W/W K .We should not try to compose cosets in Ŵ k\W/W ̂ 0.

Remark 2.28. In [EK23] a category is introduced where the objects are finitary parabolic subsets and the morphisms are double cosets. Multiplication of distinguished representatives agrees with composition of cosets in this category only when the composition is reduced. To avoid introducing additional technical overhead from [EK23], we focus on reduced expressions in the next section.

In some sense, this proof boils down to the additivity of λ 7 → ~cλ, see Theorem 2.20. 2.5. Reduced expressions. Let us briefly recall the concept of reduced expressions for double cosets. We refer the reader to [EK23] for a comprehensive exploration of this idea. 

Definition 2.30. Fix a Coxeter system ( W, S ). A (singular) multistep expression is the data of a sequence of finitary subsets of S of the form I0 ⊂ K1 ⊃ I1 ⊂ . . . ⊂ Kd ⊃ Id. We shall use the notation 

IK • = [[ I0 ⊂ K1 ⊃ I1 ⊂ . . . ⊂ Kd ⊃ Id]] (2.31) to refer to singular multistep expressions. 16 QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 

A (singular) singlestep expression is the data of a sequence of finitary subsets I0, I 1, . . . , I d,where for each index 0 ≤ i < d , the subsets Ii and Ii+1 differ by the addition or subtraction of a single element. We use the notation 

I• = [ I0, I 1, . . . , I d]. (2.32) We also use notation like [ I0 + s − t + u . . . ] to keep track of the simple reflection added or subtracted at each step. 

Example 2.31. In the affine Weyl group it is more common to keep track of which simple reflections are absent, rather than which are present. There is a singlestep expression [̂2,̂ 02 ,̂ 024 ,̂ 04 ,̂ 034 ,̂ 34] = [ ̂2 − 0 − 4 + 2 − 3 + 0] . (2.33) Forgetting the order in which 0 and 4 are subtracted, we obtain the multistep expression [[ ̂2 ⊃̂ 024 ⊂̂ 04 ⊃̂ 034 ⊂̂ 34]] . (2.34) This may not appear exactly like the form of (2.31), e.g. starting with ⊃ rather than ⊂, but keep in mind that equalities are permitted in (2.31). A more pedantic version of this multistep expression would be [[ ̂2 ⊂̂ 2 ⊃̂ 024 ⊂̂ 04 ⊃̂ 034 ⊂̂ 34 ⊃̂ 34]] .

Meanwhile, the singlestep expression [ ̂2 − 4 − 0 + 2 − 3 + 0] is distinct from the one in (2.33), though it is adapted to the same multistep expression (2.34). 

Remark 2.32. There are many multistep expressions adapted to a single step expression (allowing equalities willy nilly), and a finite number of single-step expressions adapted to each multistep expression (choosing the order in which simple reflections are added or subtracted). Ultimately, in the language of [EK23], all of these will be expressions for the same double coset, and if one of them is a reduced expression then they all are. We write I• ⇌ IK • when a singlestep expression is adapted to a multistep expression. Later in this paper, singlestep expressions will take on the leading role as they parametrize objects in the diagrammatic 2-category D, but it is often more efficient to use multistep expressions for combinatorial definitions.

To summarize, one should think that to each piece [[ K ⊃ I]] of a multistep expression one has the element wK wI , which is the “distinguished representative” of the double coset in 

WK \W/W I containing the identity. When the expression is reduced, then taking the product of these distinguished representatives over a multistep expression one obtains the distinguished representative pw Id of the coset p being expressed, and the lengths add in this product.

Example 2.36. For any a 6 = b ∈ Ω, [[ ̂a ⊃̂ a, b ⊂̂ a]] is not a reduced expression, as w = ŵaŵ

> a,b

is not minimal in wW ̂ a.2.6. Reduced expressions for dominant weights. Definition 2.37. A fundword is a finite sequence ( ̟ j1 , . . . ,̟ jd ) of fundamental weights. If 

∑dr=1 ̟ jr = λ we call the fundword a reduced expression or rex for λ, and we often use the notation λ = ( ̟ j1 , . . . ,̟ jd ). 

Definition 2.38. Fix a fundword λ = ( ̟ j1 , . . . ,̟ jd ) for λ ∈ Λ+weight . A sequence of elements 

a0, a 1, . . . , a d ∈ Ω is adapted to λ if ar−1 = ar + jr for all 1 ≤ r ≤ d, in which we call this sequence a coloring . Since the choice of a single ai determines the rest, and the first and last entries a0 and ad have special importance, we denote the choice of λ together with a coloring as (a0, λ ) or ( a0, λ, a d) or ( λ, a d).

Such a choice is called a colored fundword . To a colored fundword, we define the corresponding singlestep expression GS( λ, a d) as the concatenation of the expressions [ ̂ar−1 ⊃̂ ar−1, a r ⊂̂ ar]for 1 ≤ r ≤ d.That is, we concatenate the reduced expressions for p̟ jr , appropriately rotated, to get the singular expression associated with λ.

Example 2.39. Let λ = ( ̟ 2,̟ 1,̟ 3,̟ 2) and a0 = 11. Then GS(11 , λ ) = GS( λ, 3) = [ ̂11 ⊃̂ 9, 11 ⊂̂ 9 ⊃̂ 9, 8 ⊂̂ 8 ⊃̂ 5, 8 ⊂̂ 5 ⊃̂ 3, 5 ⊂̂ 3] . (2.39) For this example the value of n is irrelevant so long as n ≥ 4 (in order that ̟ 3 makes sense). 

Remark 2.40. Colored fundwords ( λ, a d) will parametrize the 1-morphisms of Webs Ω, while singlestep expressions will parametrize 1-morphisms of D, and the 2-functor GS will send (λ, a d) 7 → GS( λ, a d). This justifies the use of GS in our notation. The following theorem is effectively just Theorem 2.29 with different terminology.

2.7. Extended affine Weyl groups. We aim to match the combinatorics above with some standard conventions. For a complex reductive algebraic group G with a pinning T ⊂ B ⊂ G, we have the extended affine Weyl group defined as follows. Let χ = χ∗(T ) denote the cocharacter lattice 6

of T , χroot ⊂ χ the coroot lattice. Let Wf denote the (finite) Weyl group, thought of as a subgroup of Aut( χ). 3. SSBim from Affine Type A Realization 

We recall the background material for setting up the algebraic category SSBim of singular Soergel bimodules from a given realization. We then recall the categorification results and the Williamson-Soergel Hom formula, and then show that these results are valid for the deformed affine type A realization we are working with, under suitable assumptions on the ground field. We also briefly discuss a reformulation of the classical Satake isomorphism, which is categorified by the the functor GS ζ constructed in Subsection 5.4. 3.1. Deformed affine realizations. Definition 3.1. (See [EW16, Definition 3.1]) Let ( W, S ) be a Coxeter system and k a com-mutative domain. Definition 3.6. We have the rotation operator σ, which is the Dynkin diagram automorphism 

σ : Ω → Ω : i 7 → i + 1. It acts on Λ ζ by σ(xi) = xσ(i) and σ(ζ) = ζ. We also have the reflection operator τ : Ω → Ω : i 7 → − i. It acts on Λ ζ by τ (xi) = x1−i and τ (ζ) = ζ−1. Note that τ does not preserve the roots; it satisfies τ (αi) = −ζ−1ατ (i).Note that τ fixes the affine vertex, and acts on the finite Weyl group by its traditional Dynkin automorphism (conjugation by the longest element). However, the isomorphism of Lemma 3.5 does not quite intertwine τ with the action of the longest element, being off by a multiplicative factor of ζn−1.3.2. Balancedness. We recall the concept of balancedness. 

Definition 3.7. ([Eli16, Definition A.4]) Let ( W, S ) be a Coxeter system equipped with a realization, and let s, t ∈ S. It is proven in [Eli16, §A.2] that, whenever mst = 2 k + 1 is odd, one has (st )k(αs) = λα t, (ts )k(αt) = λ−1αs, (3.5) for some scalar λ ∈ k×. The realization is balanced for {s, t } if λ = 1. There is a separate definition for mst even that we do not recall here, and it is vacuous when mst = 2. We say the realization is balanced if it is balanced for all pairs {s, t } with mst < ∞.When mst = 3, being balanced for {s, t } is equivalent to the corresponding entries in the Cartan matrix being −1. The deformed affine realization is not balanced. Under standard notions of what “positive roots” should mean, two colinear positive roots should be equal, and both αt and ( st )k(αs) should be positive roots. These two requirements are in conflict when the realization is not balanced. 3.3. Singular Soergel Bimodules from Realizations. In [Soe07], Soergel constructed a category SBim of bimodules to categorify the Hecke algebra, starting from a nice representation of a Coxeter group ( W, S ). Williamson, in [Wil11], generalized this construction to obtain a 2-category 7 SSBim of singular Soergel bimodules to categorify the Hecke algebroid 8. We recall these constructions over the next few sections. Given a realization (Λ , Λ∗, {αs}, {α∨ 

> s

RI := RWI = {f ∈ R : s(f ) = f for all s ∈ I}.

The Hecke algebroid associated to a Coxeter system ( W, S ) is a relative version of the Hecke algebra associated to all pairs of finitary subsets I, J ⊂ S. S. We now go into the details of the construction. Let H denote the Hecke algebra associated to ( W, S ). This is an algebra over Z[v, v −1]. Let

Categorification results and Hom formula. In section 3.3, we defined (following [Wil11]) a 2-category constructed from a given realization of a Coxeter group ( W, S ). Under nice enough assumptions (as in [Wil11]), or under a suitable modification of SSBim as in [Abe25] (see Theorem 3.53), the so-called Soergel-Williamson categorification theorem and Hom formula both hold. These assumptions do not hold for the deformed affine realization, so we will need to extend the known results. Let us first recall the results in the literature, and discuss assumptions afterwards. Throughout this section, we shall assume that the base k is an infinite field.

Whenever I ⊂ J, [[ I ⊂ J]] is a reduced expression for the double coset p containing the identity, and the induction bimodule BS( I ⊂ J) := BS([[ I ⊂ J]]) = RI is indecomposable, so equals Bp. We have I ch J (Bp) = I HJp = HwJ . Similar statements can be made for [[ J ⊃ I]] and the shifted restriction bimodule, which has character HwJ as well. The categorification theorem sends the generators of SSBim to the standard generators of the Hecke algebroid. Property (1) above then implies that the character of BS( I•) is bs( I•) for any singular expression I•.

Remark 3.18. For certain nice realizations (typically in characteristic zero) the Soergel con-jecture holds, which states that I ch J (Bp) = I HJp for all p. Remark 3.24. On the other side of the Soergel Satake equivalence, the corresponding state-ment is dim End( L̟ k−1 ⊗ L̟ 1 ) = 2 . (3.19) One typically proves this using Schur’s lemma and the decomposition

Note however that (3.19) holds even in finite characteristic where (3.20) might fail! Similarly, the decomposition BS( I•) ∼= Bψ0(̟ 1+̟ k−1) ⊕ Bψ0(̟ k ) (3.21) need not hold in finite characteristic. We will not rely on such decompositions in this paper, only on the decategorified statements and their implications for the size of morphism spaces. 3.6. The Satake isomorphism in terms of the Hecke algebroid. In this section, we rephrase the Satake isomorphism (which was categorified into the geometric Satake equiv-alence) as an isomorphism between the Grothendieck group of Rep( SL n) and a version of the spherical Hecke algebra for Wext constructed within the Hecke algebroid. We then define Rep Ω(SL n) (or rather, its quantum analogue), and reinterpret the the Satake isomorphism as a functor into the Hecke algebroid. We retain our notation from Section 2, so that Λ weight denotes the weight lattice of sl n,Λ+weight denotes the subset of dominant weights, sh : Λ weight → Ω denotes the quotient map with respect to the root lattice, and ψa denotes the bijection in Theorem 2.27. Let Z[Λ weight ]denote the the group algebra of Λ weight . It is well known that [Rep(SL n)] = Z[Λ weight ]W , the subring of W -invariants. One one side of the Satake isomorphism is [Rep(SL n)] ⊗Z Z[v±1] = 

Z[v±1][Λ weight ]W .

There is a decomposition Rep( SL n) = ⊕

We do not discuss the spherical Hecke algebra here, but instead work within the Hecke algebroid associated to W .28 QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE

The algebra structure on H may appear slightly artificial, hence we prefer to work directly with the Hecke algebroid, and do not discriminate between maximal finitary subsets of S.

Definition 3.27. The category KΩ is defined as follows. 

• The objects of KΩ are elements of Ω = Z/n Z.

• The morphism space Hom( l, k ) is the Z[Λ weight ]Wk−l, spanned by the characters of rep-resentations whose weights live in the root coset k − l ∈ Ω. Composition is given by multiplication within Z[Λ weight ]W .

This is naturally a Z-linear category. We denote by KΩ⊗ZZ[v±1] the Z[v±1]-linear category with same objects, but with morphism spaces given by Hom KΩ (l, k ) ⊗Z Z[v±1] = Z[v±1][Λ weight ]Wk .The following theorem is equivalent Theorem 3.26, after translating the unnatural multipli-cation of (3.24) into the more natural multiplication in the Hecke algebroid H .

Theorem 3.28. There is a functor KΩ → H which sends the object k ∈ Ω to the maximal finitary subset ̂ k, and sends ch( Lλ) ∈ Hom( l, k ) to ̂ kĤ lψl(λ) ∈̂ kĤ l. The induced functor 

KΩ ⊗Z Z[v±1] → H

is fully faithful, with essential image the set of maximal finitary subsets of S.

Remark 3.30. In [Eli17], the set of objects is taken to be the set of “removable” vertices of the Dynkin diagram, which is a torsor over Ω. thereof, constructed in Subsection 5.4. 3.7. Assumptions on a realization. Now we discuss the assumptions made by Williamson in his proofs of these results, and how to weaken those assumptions. First we recall the notion of reflection faithfulness for a representation V of a Coxeter group ( W, I ) as introduced in [Soe07] and later used in [Wil11]. 

Definition 3.32. Let ( W, S ) be a Coxeter system, and k be a field. A reflection faithful 

representation of W is a finite dimensional representation V of W over k such that: 

• The representation is faithful; 

• We have codim V w = 1 if and only if w is a reflection. 

Lemma 3.33. Let char k 6 = 2 and let V be a reflection faithful representation of (W, S ). Then for any reflection t ∈ W , there exists a pair (ht, v t) ∈ V ∗ × V such that 

t(λ) = λ − 2ht(λ)vt

for all λ ∈ V . The pair (ht, v t) is unique up to the equivalence relation (ht, v t) ∼ (ah t, a −1vt)

for units a ∈ k×.Proof. When char k is not 2, any involution acts diagonalizably with eigenvalues 1 and −1. When V is a reflection faithful representation of W and t is a reflection, the second condition in Theorem 3.32 then implies that V has a one dimensional −1-eigenspace for t. Choose any non-zero element vt in the −1-eigenspace, and let ht ∈ V ∗ be the unique element which vanishes on V t and takes the value 1 on vt. By verifying the statement for V t and for vt, one concludes that t(λ) = λ − 2ht(λ)vt. The uniqueness statement is straightforward. 

Of course, one should think of ( ht, v t) as a root and (half a) coroot attached to t.Let T denote the set of reflections in W . For t, t ′ ∈ T , fix some ht, h t′ as in the above lemma. If ht, h t′ are not linearly independent, then V t = ker( ht) = ker( ht′ ) = V t′

, so that 30 QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 

codim V tt ′

≤ 1. Since tt ′ is not a reflection, reflection faithfulness of V implies that V tt ′

= V ,and we have that t = t′ by faithfulness of the representation V . Hence we have the following lemma ([Wil11, Lemma 4.1.4]): 

Lemma 3.34. Let char k 6 = 2 and V be a reflection faithful representation of (W, S ). The elements of {ht : t ∈ T } ⊂ V ∗ as above are pairwise linearly independent. 

Theorems 3.17 and 3.21 as proven in [Wil11] assume that the following properties hold. 

Property 3.35. The following are properties of a finite-dimensional realization Λ of ( W, S )over a field k. As usual, we regard Λ ∗ as a W -module via the contragredient action, and let 

R = Sym k(Λ). (1) Λ ∗ is a reflection faithful representation of ( W, S ), and k is an infinite field of charac-teristic 6 = 2. (2) For all finitary I ⊂ S, R is graded free over RI , and one has an isomorphism of graded

Remark 3.36. The statement of Theorem 3.35 (1) was phrased in terms of Λ ∗ rather than Λ to be consistent with Williamson’s conventions. Regardless, when comparing with Lie theory, the roots (not the coroots) should be elements of R. It is not hard to verify that (1) holds for Λ∗ if and only if it holds for Λ. The same can be said for (3), but we are not sure whether the same can be said for (2). 

Remark 3.37. One can think of Theorem 3.35 (3) as the statement that the realization has something like a “non-integral root system.” In [Wil11, Section 4.1], both Theorem 3.35 (1) and (2) are stated as explicit assumptions, but (3) is merely asserted as a property which holds. This assertion is incorrect, so we view this property as a third assumption made by Williamson. 

Lemma 3.38. Property (3) fails for the deformed affine realization. Proof. Suppose that (3) holds, and choose a family of elements ( ht, v t) for each reflection. Note that hs1 must be in the span of x1 − ζx 2; by rescaling the entire family we can assume 

hs1 = x1 − ζx 2. Conjugating s1 by s1s2 yields, s2, so we apply s1s2 to hs1 and deduce that 

hs2 = ζx 1 − ζ2x2. Continuing to apply sisi+1 to hsi , we deduce that hsn+1 = ζnx1 − ζn+1 x2.But sn+1 = s1, a contradiction. 

Let us state a weaker property which a realization might possess. 

Property 3.39. We continue the setup of Theorem 3.35. (3’) One may choose a family of pairs {(ht, v t) : t ∈ T } as in Theorem 3.33, such that 

xh s ∈ k× · ht if xsx −1 = t

where k× denotes the group of units in k.

Lemma 3.40. Property (1) implies Property (3’). QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 31 

Proof. In fact, one can choose any pairs ( ht, v t) for each reflection t, and Property (3’) will hold. By Theorem 3.33, one need only show that xh s is a −1-eigenvector for t, which is obvious. 

We shall see in section 3.8 that our deformed affine realization satisfies assumptions (1), (2), and thus also (3’), when working over k = Q(ζ) for an indeterminate ζ.

since we can think of mp as a product of the s-invariant elements hthsts . We claim that hthsts is still s-invariant with assumption (3’). Indeed, by (3’), we have that s(hsts ) = λh t for some λ ∈ k×. Then hsts = λs (ht), so that s(ht) = λ−1hsts . It follows that 

s(hthsts ) = s(ht)s(hsts ) = λ−1hsts λh t = hthsts . 

Hence we conclude that Theorems 3.17 and 3.21 hold without requiring (3). 

Theorem 3.42. Theorems 3.17 and 3.21 hold when one only requires Properties (1) and (2) but not (3). 

3.8. The deformed affine Type A realization. In this section, we discuss the deformed affine Type A realization (Theorem 3.4) in more detail, and prove that Properties (1) and (2) from section 3.5 hold when the ground field is Q(ζ), so that we can use the categorification theorem and Hom formula from section 3.5. In preparation for future work, we aim to establish this result in reasonable generality. For this purpose it will be helpful to think of realizations over integral domains that are not necessarily fields. Since both [Wil11], [Soe07] define “reflection faithfulness” only over fields (see Definition 3.32), we give a generalized definition. There are many generalizations which will specialize to the definition in [Wil11, Soe07], but this one is sufficient for our purposes. 

Definition 3.43. V is a reflection faithful representation of ( W, S ) over an integral domain A

if: (a) V is a faithful representation, (b) V w is free of finite rank over A for all w ∈ W ,(c) V /V w is free of finite rank over A for all w ∈ W (i.e., V w is a direct summand of V ), (d) rk A(V /V w) = 1 if and only if w is a reflection in W .34 QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 

Lemma 3.44. Reflection faithfulness is stable under flat base change, i.e., if A → B is a flat morphism of commutative domains and V a reflection faithful representation of (W, S ) over 

A, then VB := V ⊗A B is a reflection faithful representation over B.Proof. Observe that for w ∈ W ,

−−−→ V ).

−−−−−−−→ V ⊗A B) = V w ⊗A B, VB /(VB )w = Im( V ⊗A B (w−1) ⊗1B

−−−−−−−→ V ⊗A B) = ( V /V w) ⊗A B

and (a), (b), (c) and (d) in Definition 3.43 follow. 

The following lemma helps us compute fixed point sets. The product of a diagonal matrix and a permutation matrix is called a rescaled permutation matrix .

Lemma 3.48. Let ΛA be a specialization of Λζ as in Theorem 3.4. For any element y ∈ W ,the fixed point set ΛyA and the quotient ΛA/ΛyA are both free modules over A. The rank of ΛyA

over A agrees with the rank of Λyζ over Z[ζ, ζ −1] so long as ζ ∈ A× has infinite order. Proof. By Theorem 3.47, y acts by a rescaled permutation matrix. From Theorem 3.45, we deduce the freeness of V yA and VA/V yA . The entries of t are powers of ζ, so the element c for any cycle is a power of ζ. By the infinite order assumption, c = 1 in A if and only if c = 1 in 

Z[ζ, ζ −1]. 

Theorem 3.49. The realization Λζ is a reflection-faithful representation of W . Let A be any 

Z[ζ, ζ −1]-algebra for which the unit ζ ∈ A has infinite order. Then ΛA is reflection faithful as well. Proof. Faithfulness of VA is an immediate consequence of Theorem 3.47 and Theorem 3.45 (for 

w ∈ Sn, λ ∈ Λroot , the only way wt λ acts as the identity matrix is if w = 1 and λ = 0, by the explicit description of the corresponding matrices in Theorem 3.47 and the argument in Theorem 3.45). We are left to show that VA is reflection faithful, i.e., we have to show that (b),(c) and (d) in Definition 3.43 hold. Theorem 3.48 shows us that (b) and (c) hold, so now we only have to show that (d) holds. By Theorem 3.45 the fixed point set of any simple reflection s has codimension 1. The same is true for any reflection, as the fixed point set of wsw −1 is w(Λ sA). On the other hand, suppose we have w = w′tλ ∈ W with w′ ∈ Sn, λ ∈ Λroot such that rk A(Λ wA) = n − 1. By Theorem 3.45, w′ needs to have at least n − 1 cycles, so either w′ is a reflection in Sn, or w′ is the identity. If w′ is the identity, so that w = tλ, then we need exactly n − 1 diagonal entries in ρ(tλ) (see Theorem 3.47) to be 1, so that all but one cycle is monodromy-free. But ρ(tλ) has determinant 1, so the last entry is also 1, a contradiction. Thus w′ is a reflection. Suppose n ≥ 3. By further conjugating w (and changing the meaning of λ) we may assume that w = s1tλ. By Theorem 3.45, all n − 1 cycles of s1 must be monodromy-free. In particular, all diagonal entries in ρ(tλ) must be 1 except the first two, from which one deduces that λ is a multiple of α1. Thus w = s1tkα 1 for some k ∈ Z, and w is a reflection. We leave to the reader the analogous proof when n = 2. 



Remark 3.53. Noriyuke Abe in [Abe25] generalizes the construction of [Wil11] by starting with weaker assumptions, including cases where V is not a faithful representation of ( W, S )but is a faithful representation for all finitary subgroups, and allowing k to be a complete local Noetherian integral domain. Let Q denote the fraction field of R, and QI the WI -invariant subring. Now we define SSBim Abe as follows: instead of just working with ( RI , R J )-bimodules 

M , one has the extra data of certain ( QI , Q J )-bimodules ( M xQ)x∈WI \W/W J and isomorphisms 

QI ⊗RI M ⊗RJ QJ ≃ ⊕  

> x∈WI\W/W J

M xQ which satisfy certain conditions. See [Abe25, section 2] for further details of this construction. It is shown then in [Abe25] that Theorems 3.17 and Theorems 3.21 hold under his weaker assumptions for SSBim Abe . Abe is careful not to rely on the balanced assumption. We expect Abe’s construction to be useful when studying specializations of Λ ζ where ζ is a root of unity, as such specializations are no longer faithful. 3.9. Reverse Bott-Samelsons. The goal of this section is to prove that the 2-functor GS is essentially surjective after taking the Karoubi envelope. A brief pedagogical interlude on ordinary Bott-Samelson bimodules will set the stage. We work with a realization satisfying Assumptions (1) and (2). The traditional category of Bott-Samelson bimodules BS Bim (these are ( R, R )-bimodules) is the full subcategory of SBS Bim( ∅, ∅) only containing objects associated to singular expressions of the form [[ ∅ ⊂ { s1} ⊃ ∅ ⊂ { s2} ⊃ · · · ⊂ { sd} ⊃ ∅ ]] . (3.32) QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 39 

Thus BS Bim and SBS Bim( ∅, ∅) are not the same. However, these two monoidal categories have the same Karoubi envelope, which is not obvious, but follows from the classification of indecomposables in each category. This is stated as [EMTW20, Theorem 24.12] but the proof is not explained, so let us elaborate. 

Theorem 3.54. The Karoubi envelopes of (the graded, additive completions of) BS Bim and 

SBS Bim (∅, ∅) are equivalent. Proof. Since BS Bim ⊂ S BS Bim( ∅, ∅) we need only prove that every indecomposable summand of a singular Bott-Samelson bimodule in SBS Bim( ∅, ∅) is isomorphic to a summand of an ordi-nary Bott-Samelson bimodule. By Theorem 3.17, the isomorphism classes of indecomposable objects in I SJ are in bijection with double cosets p ∈ WI \W/W J , and appears as a direct summand of BS( IK •) whenever IK • is a (singular) reduced expression for p.When I = J = ∅, p = {w} is a singleton, and any ordinary reduced expression for w gives rise to singular reduced expression as in (3.32) (though not all singular reduced expressions are of this form). Consequently, the indecomposable bimodule Bp is a summand of an ordinary Bott-Samelson bimodule (it would traditionally be called Bw). 

Ultimately, the basic combinatorial fact underlying this theorem is: every double coset 

p ∈ W∅\W/W ∅ has a singular reduced expression of the form (3.32). Ordinary Bott-Samelson bimodules bounce between minimally singular and subminimally singular. In this paper, we mostly deal with the other extreme. Recall the definition of a maximal double coset from Theorem 2.2. 

Definition 3.55. Let ( W, S ) be an affine Weyl group equipped with a realization satisfying Assumptions (1) and (2). The 2-category of maximally singular Soergel bimodules , mSSBim denote the 1-full sub-2-category 11 of SSBim whose objects are the maximal finitary subsets ̂ s of 

S. The 2-category of reverse Bott-Samelson bimodules mSBS Bim is the 2-full sub-2-category 12 

of mSSBim containing 1-morphisms of the form [[ ̂a1 ⊃̂ a1a2 ⊂̂ a2 ⊃̂ a2a3 ⊂ · · · ⊃ ̂ ad−1ad ⊂̂ ad]] . (3.33) To avoid redundancy we assume that aj 6 = aj+1 for all 1 ≤ j < d .

Theorem 3.56. In affine type A, the Karoubi envelope of (the graded, additive completion of) mSBS Bim is equivalent to mSSBim. Moreover, every expression of the form (3.33) is a reduced expression. Proof. Since mSBS Bim ⊂ mSSBim, we need to prove that every indecomposable singular Bott-Samelson bimodule in ̂ sŜt is a summand of a reverse Bott-Samelson bimodule (for all 

s, t ∈ S). Just as in the proof of Theorem 3.54, Williamson’s categorification theorem reduces this problem to proving that every maximal double coset has a singular reduced expression of the form (3.33). Every expression (3.33) is GS( λ, a d) for some λ, whence it is reduced by Theorem 2.41. Fixing the parabolic subgroup ̂ ad on the right, the bijections (2.26) show that any maximal double coset has the form ψad (λ) for some λ ∈ Λ+weight , and Theorem 2.41 provides many reduced expressions of the desired form (associated to rexes λ for λ). 

Remark 3.57. The Bott-Samelson bimodules for the reduced expressions from Theorem 2.41 are the images under geometric Satake of tensor products of fundamental representations.

The analogous statement to Theorem 3.56 on the other side of the Soergelified Satake equiva-lence is that Rep( sl n) is equivalent to the Karoubi envelope of Fund( sl n), the full subcategory monoidally generated by fundamental representations. The corresponding statement under geometric Satake states that the corresponding reverse (singular) Bott-Samelson variety gives a resolution of singularities of a Schubert variety associated to the corresponding dominant weight within the affine Grassmannian. 4. Frobenius Data and Calculations with Demazure Operators 

We recall the notion of Frobenius extensions, Demazure operators, and Frobenius hyper-cubes. These will be used in the next chapter to construct a diagrammatic category as in [EWS16]. This chapter ends with various calculations involving Demazure operators for the deformed affine realization. These calculations will be used in section 6. 4.1. Cubes of Frobenius Extensions. Definition 4.1. A graded (commutative) Frobenius extension of degree d is an extension of commutative graded rings ι : ֒A → B, where B is a finitely generated free graded A-module, equipped with an A-linear map ∂ = ∂BA : B → A of degree −2d, called the (Frobenius) trace .In other words, ∂ is a homomorphism of graded A-modules ∂ : B → A(−2d). The trace is required to be non-degenerate , meaning the following map is an isomorphism: 

B → Hom A(B, A ) : b 7 → (b′ 7 → ∂(bb ′)) .

Remark 4.2. The non-degeneracy of ∂ is equivalent to requiring that B admits a pair of dual bases . This is a pair of bases {xi} and {yi} for B as an A-module, such that ∂(xiyj ) = δij . The equivalence between these two notions is familiar from linear algebra. Within Hom A(B, A )there is a dual basis {x∗ 

> i

} to the basis {xi}, and one takes yi to be the preimage of x∗ 

> i

under 

B → Hom A(B, A ). If ∂ is non-degenerate then any basis {xi} admits a unique dual basis. If we start with a homogeneous basis {xi} ⊂ B, then the dual basis {yi} is also homogeneous. After all, if {yi} is the dual basis to {xi}, then the appropriate homogeneous components of 

{yi} are also dual to {xi}, but the dual basis is unique. The data of a Frobenius extension also equips one with a comultiplication map ∆ BA : B →

B ⊗A B, which is a B-bimodule map. It sends 1 7 → ∑ 

> α

xi ⊗yi, an element which is independent of the choice of dual bases. If ֒A → B is a graded Frobenius extension of degree d, then the comultiplication map ∆ BA has degree 2 d.

Definition 4.3. The product-coproduct element μBA of a Frobenius extension ֒A → B is defined to be 

μBA := mBA ◦ ∆BA (1) where mBA : B ⊗A B → B denotes the multiplication map of B.

Lemma 4.4. The element ∂BA (μBA ) ∈ A is a scalar, equal to the rank (not the graded rank) of 

B as an A-module. Proof. This follows directly from the definition of dual bases. 

Being a Frobenius extension is a structure (the choice of ∂), not just a property. However, the choice of ∂ is unique up to a choice of a unit in B.QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 41 

Lemma 4.5. Suppose ∂, ∂ ′ : B → A are two Frobenius traces. Then there exists a unit b0 ∈ B

such that ∂′(b) = ∂(b0b) for all b ∈ B. If μ and μ′ represent μBA for these two choices of Frobenius structure, then μ = b0μ′.Thus the product-coproduct element is independent of the choice of Frobenius trace up to unit, and if μ = μ′ then ∂ = ∂′.Proof. Via the isomorphism 

B → Hom A(B, A ) : b 7 → (b′ 7 → ∂(bb ′)) ,

we have that ∂′ ∈ Hom A(B, A ) corresponds to some b0 ∈ B, so that ∂′(b) = ∂(b0b) for any 

b ∈ B. We leave the reader to verify that b0 is a unit, and the remaining statements. 

Of note is the case when ֒A → B induces an equality A× = B×, because then ∂′ = a0∂ for 

a0 ∈ A×. We say ∂′ is a rescaling of ∂. For example, in this paper it happens frequently that 

A and B are k-algebras and A× = B× = k×.Note that if A ⊂ B and B ⊂ C are Frobenius extensions, then A ⊂ C is a Frobenius extension as well, with trace ∂CA = ∂BA ∂CB . It is proven in [EWS16, (2.5)] that 

μCA = μCB μBA . (4.1) 

Definition 4.6. A hypercube of (graded) Frobenius extensions or a Frobenius hypercube will be the following datum. 

• A finite set Γ. We also use Γ to designate the entire datum. We consider the hypercube with vertices labelled by subsets of Γ. An edge in this hypercube corresponds to I\γ ⊂ I

for some γ ∈ I ⊂ Γ, and parallel edges correspond to the same γ.

• A (contravariant) assignment of graded rings RI to vertices in the cube, so that I ⊂

J =⇒ RJ ⊂ RI .

• For each edge, a trace map ∂I\γI : RI\γ → RI making RI֒ → RI\γ into a graded Frobenius extension. We call this hypercube compatible if, for every square I ⊂ J, J ′ ⊂ K in the hypercube, we have ∂JK ∂IJ = ∂J′ 

> K

∂IJ′ . In this case, there is a well-defined map ∂IJ : RI → RJ for every I ⊂ J,which endows the extension RJ ⊂ RI with the structure of a Frobenius extension. We assume henceforth that every hypercube of Frobenius extensions is compatible. We usually refer to R∅ as just R. Similarly we write ∂I := ∂∅ 

> I

.A partial hypercube of Frobenius extensions or a partial Frobenius hypercube is a finite set Γ and a non-empty collection F of subsets of Γ (i.e. vertices in the hypercube), together with the data of graded rings and Frobenius extensions as above but only for vertices within the subset F. We require that J ∈ F and I ⊂ J implies I ∈ F . Note that ∅ is always in F. For the purposes of this paper, we shall use the term Frobenius hypercube to also to refer to partial Frobenius hypercubes. For a Frobenius hypercube and I ⊂ J ∈ F , let μIJ ∈ RI denote the product-coproduct element for the Frobenius extension RJ ⊂ RI , and μI = μ∅ 

> I

. By (4.1) we have 

μIJ = μJ

μI

. (4.2) It is straightforward to see that (4.2) is equivalent to compatibility. For sake of simplicity, assume that all rings in the hypercube are k-algebras and that their unit groups agree with k×. By Theorem 4.5, each individual Frobenius extension I ⊂ J in the cube is uniquely determined up to a scalar, and this scalar is visible in the choice of μIJ . When defining a new Frobenius hypercube structure by rescaling the various elements μIJ , constraints 42 QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 

are placed on these scaling factors by (4.2). Ultimately, one can freely rescale the elements 

μI ∈ R (resp. the trace maps ∂I ) for each I ∈ F , and there will be a unique way to extend this rescaling to the entire hypercube so that compatibility will be maintained. Fix a Coxeter system ( W, S ) with a realization, and let R be as in Subsection 3.3. Let Γ = S,and F be the collection of finitary subsets of S. Under some assumptions on the realization, there will be a Frobenius hypercube with rings RI associated to each I ∈ F . For example, when W is a Weyl group with its geometric realization over R, there is a standard Frobenius hypercube structure where μI is the product of the positive roots for the parabolic subgroup 

WI . However, as we have noted in §3.2, some realizations do not have a well-behaved notion of positive roots. Consequently, the choice of product-coproduct elements μI , or equivalently the choice of trace maps ∂I , will be subtle. To construct the trace maps, we need to study Demazure operators. 4.2. Demazure operators. For the rest of this section we fix a Coxeter system ( W, S ), with a realization (Λ , Λ∨, {αs}, {α∨ 

> s

}) over an integral domain k. We have the graded k-algebra 

R := Sym k Λ, where Λ is in degree 2, with its natural W -action. We define the Demazure operator or the divided difference operator ∂s associated to a simple reflection s ∈ S to be a k-linear operator on R:

∂s(f ) = f − s(f )

αs

for f ∈ R. 

Note that ∂s may also be defined as the unique k−linear operator on R satisfying the following properties: 

• ∂s|Λ coincides with the functional defined by α∨ 

> s

, i.e., ∂s(λ) = 〈α∨ 

> s

, λ 〉 for λ ∈ Λ. 

• ∂s satisfies the twisted Leibniz rule: 

∂s(f g ) = ∂s(f )g + s(f )∂s(g). (4.3) 

Lemma 4.7. ([Eli16, Claim A.7] ) The Demazure operators ∂s, ∂ t associated to simple reflec-tions s, t ∈ S satisfy the following relations. (1) ∂s∂s = 0 .(2) For mst < ∞,

λ∂ s∂t∂s · · · = ∂t∂s∂t · · · (4.4) 

(both expressions alternate and have length mst ) for some unit λ ∈ k×. Moreover, 

λ = 1 when mst is even. The last property states that the braid relations hold up to scalar. This unit λ agrees with the unit λ in (3.5) , so that λ = 1 when the realization is balanced. 

Remark 4.8. Let us fill in more details of the sketched proof from [Eli16, Claim A.7]. We can write each ∂s (and compositions thereof) as a Q-linear combination of elements of W , where 

Q is the fraction field of R. For example, when mst = 3, we have 

∂s∂t∂s(f ) = (−1) 3sts (f )

αss(αt)st (αs) + . . . , ∂t∂s∂t(f ) = (−1) 3sts (f )

αtt(αs)ts (αt) + . . . . (4.5) More generally, the coefficient of ws,t in ∂s∂t · · · (the leading term) will be ( −1) mst times the reciprocal of the product of the s-aligned choice of roots as in [Eli16, Definition A.6], whereas for ∂t∂s · · · one instead uses the t-aligned choice of roots. One can compare these leading coefficients using (3.5), and verify that (4.4) holds for the leading terms. With more combinatorial work one could verify this for all terms. QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 43 

Remark 4.9. When Ws,t acts faithfully and the Chevalley-Shephard-Todd theorem holds, one can bypass this combinatorial work by arguing that any two Rs,t -linear operators of degree 

−2m are colinear, and that elements of Ws,t are linearly independent over Q.Also, note that both sides of (4.4) are zero when the action of Ws,t is not faithful. If Ws,t acts trivially, all Demazure operators are zero. Otherwise the action will factor through a smaller dihedral group, where (4.4) holds by induction, and then ∂s∂s = 0 implies the statement. 

Definition 4.10. For an expression w = ( s1, . . . , s d) of w ∈ W , we define the Demazure operator ∂w associated to it by setting 

∂w := ∂s1 . . . ∂ sd .

When the expression w is not reduced, Theorem 4.7 implies that ∂w is 0. Theorem 4.7(2),(3) together with Matsumoto’s theorem implies that ∂w is independent of the particular choice of a reduced expression w of w up to a unit in k. We pause to note a conjecture, which to our knowledge is not in the literature at this level of generality (when the braid relations hold only up to scalar). 

Conjecture 4.11. If W acts faithfully and w is a reduced expression, then ∂w is nonzero. 

We now record how Demazure operators behave for the deformed affine realization. Until the end of this subsection, ( W, S ) is the affine Weyl group, where S is in bijection with Ω = Z/n Z,and Rζ denotes the graded ring Sym Z[ζ,ζ −1](Λ ζ ) with Λ ζ in degree 2.

The action of τ on S = Ω extends naturally to an action on the set of words in S, and on

Definition 4.16. A Frobenius (hypercube) realization or Frealization is a realization equipped with a choice of a compatible partial Frobenius hypercube, with Γ = S, F the collection of finitary subsets of S, and RI the subring of WI -invariants in R for I ∈ F . In other words, we have a Frobenius structure ∂JI on RI ⊂ RJ for each finitary pair J ⊂ I ⊂ S such that 

∂JI ∂KJ = ∂KI whenever K ⊂ J. (In particular, we assume that such a Frobenius structure exists.) We also require that 

• The Frobenius trace R → Rs must simply be ∂s, as determined by the realization. 

• The Frobenius trace ∂I : R → RI must agree with ∂wI , up to a unit in k, for some reduced expression wI of wI .

Remark 4.17. The second condition, that ∂I and ∂wI agree up to unit, is automatic by Theorem 4.5 so long as ∂wI is nondegenerate. A prerequisite for ∂wI to be nondegenerate is for it to be surjective as a map R → RI , which is called generalized Demazure surjectivity 

[EKLP24, Definition 3.5]. 

Remark 4.18. It seems as though the choice of ∂s is fixed whereas the choice of ∂I is an invertible scalar by Theorem 4.5. In truth, ∂s can also be rescaled by rescaling the choice of roots and coroots (i.e. changing the underyling realization). An alternate approach would be to omit the realization from the data, and recover it from the Frobenius extension structure for Rs ⊂ R.QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 45 

Example 4.19. If the realization Λ is balanced (see §3.2) then we unambiguously define ∂w

to be ∂w for any reduced expression w of w. In this case, we can then define ∂∅ 

> I

:= ∂wI for a finitary I ⊂ S, and 

∂JI := ∂wI w−1

> J

for J ⊂ I ⊂ S. Compatibility will hold. If the realization is also faithful and satisfies gen-eralized Demazure surjectivity, then ∂JI is nondegenerate, and this data yields a Frealization. This is proven in [EKLP24, Theorem 4.3] under these exact assumptions on the realization (see [EKLP24, Section 3.1]). Our goal for the rest of this section is to define a Frobenius realization extending the deformed affine realization. Once again, ( W, S ) is the type A affine Weyl group with our standard conventions.

Definition 4.24. [EWS16, Definition 1.7] We say that a Frobenius hypercube satisfies condi-tion ⋆ if, for every I 6 = J ∈ F with I ∪ J ∈ F , setting K = I ∩ J and L = I ∪ J, we may choose a basis {xα} of RK over RI such that xα ∈ RJ .Note that in [EWS16], this is stated a bit differently, where it is required to have a pair of dual bases for RK over RI where one of the bases lives in RJ . But if we have any basis of RK

over RI already in RJ , then we can find its dual basis by Remark 4.2. The point to emphasize is that typically the dual basis will not live within RJ .

Definition 4.25. [EWS16, Definition 1.8] We say that the Frobenius hypercube Γ has no 

μ-zero divisors if μI ∈ R is not a zero divisor for all I in the hypercube Γ. 

Definition 4.26. [EWS16, Definition 1.10] We say that a Frobenius hypercube satisfies con-dition R3, if for every I ∈ F and distinct i, j, k ∈ Γ \ I with I ⊔ { i, j, k } ∈ F , we have that 

μIij μIik μIjk μI | μIijk μIi μIj μIk .QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 47 

Remark 4.27. Note that in [EWS16], the phrasing for condition R3 is slightly different. Translated to our notation, [EWS16, Definition 1.10] asks that μIIij μIIik μIIjk | μIIijk μIIi μIIj μIIk ,which is equivalent to the condition mentioned above, when there are no μ-zero divisors. As mentioned in [EWS16, Remark 1.11], when R is a UFD and μIIij , μ IIjk , μ IIik are relatively prime, the condition R3 automatically holds. 

Lemma 4.28. The deformed affine realization equipped with the Frobenius structure in Theo-rem 4.21 satsfies the conditions in Theorem 4.24, Theorem 4.25, Theorem 4.26. Proof. Clearly this Frealization has no μ-zero divisors, since each ring RI is a domain and each 

μI is nonzero. Given that each μI is defined as a product of a certain set of roots, condition R3 follows immediately from the inclusion-exclusion principle. For condition ⋆, we use similar arguments as in the proof of Theorem 4.22, passing to the permutation realization by restricting to a maximal finitary parabolic subset containing I ∪ J.The property ⋆ for the permutation realization over any commutative domain follows from [EKLP24, Theorem 4.15, Example 4.17]. The assumptions for [EKLP24, Theorem 4.15] are that the realization is faithful, balanced, and satisfies generalized Demazure surjectivity (see [EKLP24, Section 3.1]), all of which are known to hold for the permutation realization.

This section consists of various technical identities involving the Frealization structure on the deformed affine realization. We do not attempt to motivate each computation here; they will each be used in the verification of the diagrammatic Quantum Geometric Satake functor in Section 6. After Subsection 4.5.1 and Subsection 4.5.2 the reader is welcome to skip this section. 4.5.1. Quantum Numbers. For n ∈ Z≥1, we shall use the notation [ n]q to refer to the n-th quantum number explicitly given by [n]q = qn − q−n

q − q−1 = qn−1 + qn−3 + . . . q 3−n + q1−n ∈ Z[q, q −1].

We also set [0] q = 0. Thus [1] q = 1 , [2] q = q + q−1. We also have explicit formulas for product of quantum numbers, for example [2] q [n]q = [ n + 1] q + [ n − 1] q .

More generally, for m ≥ n, we have [m]q[n]q = [ n]q[m]q = [ m + n − 1] q + [ n + m − 3] q + . . . + [ m − n + 1] q .

We often drop the subscript q for convenience. For k ∈ Z≥0, we define [ k]! := [ k][ k − 1] . . . [1]. We then define the quantum binomial coefficients [nk

] to be 

[nk

]

:= [n]! [n − k]![ k]! .

For the calculations below we introduce a formal variable q satisfying 

ζn = q−2. (4.17) 50 QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE
