5. The Diagrammatic Quantum Satake Functor 

In this section, we introduce the combinatorial and diagrammatic replacements for both sides of the Quantum Geometric Satake functor: the category Webs Ω of webs, describing Fund Ω 

> q

⊂ Rep Ω(Uq(sl n)), and the category D of singular Soergel diagrams, describing SBS Bim ⊂SSBim. We then describe the Quantum Geometric Satake 2-functor in Subsection 5.4 and ad-dress fullness and faithfulness in Subsection 5.5, while also showing that it categorifies the reformulated Satake isomorphism. The proof that this 2-functor is well-defined will be post-poned to Section 6. See Theorem 5.31 for a summary of the key results. 5.1. The fundamental subcategory. Definition 5.1. Let Fund q = Fund q(sl n)) denote the full monoidal subcategory of Rep q =Rep( Uq(sl n)) monoidally generated by the fundamental representations. It is a Q(q)-linear category for a formal variable q (though not an additive category, as we have not included direct sums). The objects of Fund correspond to fundwords, in the sense of Theorem 2.37. The Karoubi envelope of Fund q is Rep q. Had we defined the analogous category over specializations of 

Z[q, q −1] to other fields, the Karoubi envelope of Fund q would agree with the category of tilting modules over that field. As explained in Subsection 3.6, Rep q is an Ω-graded monoidal category, corresponding to which we constructed a 2-category Rep Ω 

> q

in Theorem 3.29. Being a full monoidal subcategory of Rep q, Fund q is an Ω-graded-monoidal category as well. Now we define the corresponding sub-2-category of Rep Ω 

> q

.

Definition 5.2. The 2-category Fund Ω 

> q

is defined as follows: 

• The set of objects is Ω. 

• The Hom-category Hom( l, k ) is defined as Fund k−lq := Rep( Uq(sl n)) k−l ∩ Fund q.In other words, Fund Ω 

> q

is a 2-full sub-2-category of Rep Ω 

> q

where the 1-morphisms are gener-ated monoidally by the fundamental representations. The 1-morphisms of Fund Ω 

> q

correspond to colored fundwords in the sense of Theorem 2.38. 5.2. Colored sl n-Webs. A diagrammatic presentation for Fund q(sl n) over C(q) was first given by Cautis–Kamnitzer–Morrison in [CKM14]. In this paper, we use a different presentation from work of Bigelow [Big25], where the base field is also assumed to be C(q). More accurately, both [CKM14] and [Big25] describe a category which is larger than Fund q, having as generating objects both the fundamental representations and their duals. However, Fund q is equivalent to this larger category (the inclusion functor is essentially surjective), as the duals of fundamental 62 QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 

representations are isomorphic to other fundamental representations. Diagrammatically, these isomorphisms are drawn as tags. In [Big25, Proposition 1], Bigelow shows that the web relations in [CKM14] follow from this smaller collection of relations over C(q), and the proof only requires that the quantum integers are invertible. In particular, the proofs in [CKM14] and [Big25] both work over Q(q). Bigelow’s relations are those shown in fig. 1, up to taking mirror images and arrow rever-sals. While the generating morphisms in Bigelow’s presentation look the same as in Cautis– Kamnitzer–Morrison’s presentation, the meaning of some of the generating morphisms is dif-ferent; they have different conventions for the “tag.” In particular, Bigelow’s tags are defined in such a way that his webs admit both reflection symmetry and arrow reversal symmetry. The webs in [CKM14] are invariant under simultaneously reflecting and reversing arrows, but not under either individual operation. 

Remark 5.3. Bigelow “fixes” a subtle sign error in [CKM14]. The relations explicitly written in [CKM14] are correct, but the paper incorrectly asserts that the orientation-reversed relations also hold; this fails for equations (2.7) and (2.8) therein, being off by a sign, as Bigelow observed. 

Definition 5.4. The category of (Bigelow-style) sl n-webs, denoted Webs, is defined as follows. 

• The objects are generated monoidally by oriented points labelled by i ∈ { 1, 2, . . . , n −1}.

• The morphisms are generated ( Q(q)-linearly) by clockwise and counter-clockwise cups and caps, along with bivalent vertices (tags) and trivalent vertices as shown below: 

k kk kkn − kkn − k k lk + lk + lk l

• The cups and caps satisfy the isotopy relations, making the category pivotal (see [Mor07, Chapter 2] for further details.) 

• The morphisms satisfy the relations in fig. 1, and their mirror images and arrow rever-sals. As in [Big25], we shall refer to these relations in fig. 1 as “tag switching”, “tag cancellation”, “I = H”, “bursting a digon” and “bursting a square” respectively (from top to bottom, left to right). 

• It is convenient to also allow strands labeled 0 or n, with the following convention for deleting them. Any trivalent vertex that had a strand labeled 0 simply ceases to be a vertex. Any trivalent vertex that had a strand labeled n becomes a bivalent vertex, with a tag on the side of the deleted strand. 

Theorem 5.5. There is a Q(q)-linear equivalence Fund q → Webs which is not surjective on objects, but is essentially surjective. There is a fully faithful Q(q)-linear functor Webs → Rep q.The composition of these functors is the inclusion functor Fund q ⊂ Rep q.Proof. An equivalence between Cautis-Kamnitzer-Morrison’s web category and the full sub-category of Rep q generated by fundamental representations and their duals was proven in [CKM14, Theorem 3.3.1]. We merely restrict the inverse equivalence to Fund q, and translate the result through Bigelow’s equivalence. QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 63

Definition 5.9. We define 2-functors σ, rot , ΥW , rev : Webs Ω → Webs Ω, the basic symmetries of this category. On objects/region labels σ sends a 7 → a + 1. Aside from this relabling, diagrams remain unchanged. This is a monoidal, covariant equivalence. On objects rot sends a 7 → a, and it rotates all diagrams by 180 degrees. This is an anti-monoidal, contravariant equivalence. On objects Υ W sends a 7 → − a, and it flips each diagram vertically (i.e. reflecting across a horizontal axis). This is a monoidal, contravariant equivalence. On objects rev sends a 7 → − a, and it reverses all the orientations in each diagram. This is a monodial, covariant equivalence. 5.3. Diagrammatics for Singular Bott-Samelson Bimodules. In [EWS16], it was ex-plained how to construct a diagrammatic category D(Γ), an algebraic category C(Γ) and a functor Φ : D(Γ) → C (Γ), starting from a Frobenius hypercube Γ. We describe these con-structions for the (partial graded) Frobenius hypercube Γ associated to the deformed affine Frealization in Theorem 4.21. The algebraic category obtained via this construction coincides with the category SBS Bim described in Subsection 3.3 13 , hence the rest of this section is pri-marily focused on describing the diagrammatic category D(Γ) associated to the deformed affine Frealization and the functor Φ : D(Γ) → S BS Bim. There are certain relations in [EWS16] that require the additional assumptions from Theorem 4.24, Theorem 4.25, and Theorem 4.26. By Theorem 4.28, these assumptions hold for the deformed affine Frealization, so these relations are included when defining D(Γ). The construction is as follows. Let F denote the set of finitary subsets of S. The regions of 

D(Γ) are labeled by the finitary subsets I ∈ F . We typically label the regions in grey in small font, for example I for I ∈ F etc. The functor Φ sends the region labeled by I to the object 

RI in SBS Bim (defined in Subsection 3.3). For I ⊂ Γ, i, j ∈ Γ \ I, we shall use the notation Ii to refer to I ⊔ { i}, Iij to refer to 

I ⊔ { i, j } etc. The generating 1-morphisms are Ind IIi and Res IIi for each Ii ∈ F . We think of these generators as colored oriented points, where we fix a color to each i ∈ S. The orientation will be upward for Ind IIi with the region Ii to the right (and I to the left), while orientation is downward for Res IIi with region Ii to the left. It follows that the space of 1-morphisms Hom( I, J ) in D(Γ) is exactly the set of singlestep expressions as in (2.32), with I0 = J and 

Id = I.The functor Φ sends Ind IIi to the induction bimodule RI RI RIi and Res IIi to the shifted restriction bimodule RIi RI RI (ℓ(Ii ) − ℓ(I)). More generally, Φ sends a singlestep expression I•

to the singular Bott-Samelson bimodule BS( I•). The 2-morphisms are generated monoidally by oriented cups and caps (we have 4 of these for each Ii , I in F ), polynomials f ∈ RI in a region labeled I, and crossings (upward and downward) for Iij, Ij, Ii in F as shown below (where i, j are colored blue and red respec-tively). Definition 5.12. We define 2-endofunctors σ, rot , ΥS on Dpre and SBS Bim as follows. On objects/region labels σ sends I 7 → σ(I), e.g. it sends ̂ a 7 →̂ a + 1. On D, this necessarily changes the labeling on edges by applying σ to the set of simple reflections, and changes floating polynomials by applying σ on polynomials. Aside from relabeling and changing polynomials, diagrams remain unchanged. On SBS Bim, the action of σ on Hom( I, J ) is obtained from the equivalence of graded ( RI , R J )-bimodule categories induced by the isomorphism of graded algebras RI ⊗ RJ ∼

−−−→ 

> σ⊗σ

Rσ(I) ⊗ Rσ(J). This is a monoidal, covariant equivalence. On objects rot sends I 7 → I. On Dpre , it rotates all diagrams by 180 degrees and leaves floating polynomials unchanged. Note that this 2-functor coincides with combining a horizontal flip and a vertical flip in either order. On SBS Bim, rot is obtained from adjunctions, but may also be thought of as a composition of natural symmetries corresponding to horizontal and vertical flips; see Subsection 7.3 (in particular Theorem 7.28, Theorem 7.29 and Theorem 7.30) for the details. This is an antimonoidal, contravariant equivalence. On objects Υ S sends I 7 → τ (I), e.g. it sends ̂ a 7 →̂ −a. This necessarily also acts on the edge labeling and on floating polynomials by τ . It flips each diagram vertically (i.e. reflecting 70 QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 

across a horizontal axis), reverses arrows to be consistent with region labels, and acts on any floating polynomials by τ . On SBS Bim, Υ S acts on 1-morphism categories via Hom( I, J )op −→

Lemma 5.20. The 2-functor GS ζ intertwines the symmetries σ and ΥW of Webs Ω with the symmetries σ and ΥS of D respectively. It intertwines rot with rot , up to sign. More precisely, we have 

GS ζ ◦σ = σ ◦ GS ζ , GS ζ ◦ΥW = Υ S ◦ GS ζ , rot ◦ GS ζ = GS ζ ◦ rot ◦ scl , (5.29) 

where scl is the object-fixing automorphism of Webs Ω that multiplies each web by (−1) k(n−k) for each upward-oriented k in the source or target (downward-oriented strands have no effect) 14 .Proof. It suffices to check this on the generating 2-morphisms, under the assumptions in The-orem 5.17. This check is an easy exercise and is left to the reader, where Theorem 5.19 may be used to check the intertwining of Υ W and Υ S .



Remark 5.21. The symmetries are intertwined not because of any application of relations, but already at the level of the diagrams themselves. To elaborate, one could define a “free pivotal” version Webs Ω,pre of webs which have isotopy relations but no other relations. One could define a version ˜GS ζ : Webs Ω,pre → D pre of GS ζ ; this functor would still involve scalars 

λ and ν satisfying (5.25). Then Theorem 5.20 applies to ˜GS ζ as well, and descends to the corresponding result for GS ζ . We mention this point because, as is common in the field, we use symmetries to check the well-definedness of GS ζ itself. We check certain relations, and then claim others hold by applying σ or Υ or rotation. There is no fallacy in applying these symmetries before knowing of the existence of the 2-functor, because we are really applying the symmetries to ˜GS ζ .

Remark 5.22. One might hope for a symmetry of D corresponding to the arrow-reversing symmetry rev of Webs Ω. It would act on objects and 1-morphisms by τ . As evidenced by (5.24), the inward-pointing tag (with label k on bottom) is sent to an identity map times 

λk, and any autoequivalence of D sends identity maps to identity maps. Applying rev we             

> 14 For example, scl multiplies all the generators in Definition 5.4 by ( −1) k(n−k), except for the trivalent vertices which it multiplies by ( −1) k(n−k)+ l(n−l)+( k+l)( n−k−l)= 1. The 2-functor scl is isomorphic to the identity 2-functor; the natural isomorphism between them is multiplication by the appropriate sign on each object (a factor of ( −1) k(n−k)for each upward-oriented k-labeled strand). Hence rot ◦GS ζ∼= GS ζ◦rot, so that GS ζalso weakly intertwines with rotation. QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 73

We now proceed to address fullness and faithfulness of the 2-functor GS ζ .

Lemma 5.24. The images of the 2-morphisms in Rep Ω 

> q

via GS ζ have degree zero. Proof. It suffices to show that the images of the generators of Fund Ω 

> q

are of degree zero. For the images of cups (resp. caps), the degrees of the clockwise and counter-clockwise cups (resp. caps) cancel each other so that the images are of degree zero. The images of tags, being just identity maps up to a scalar, have degree zero. Finally, that the images of the trivalent vertices are of degree zero follows similarly, using Theorem 5.11 (and its analogue after applying ΥS ). 

> q

, we mean a sub-2-category of Rep Ω 

> q

which is closed under both hori-zontal (left and right) and vertical (top and bottom) compositions by arbitrary (composable) morphisms in Rep Ω 

> q

.

Lemma 5.25. The semisimple 2-category Rep Ω 

> q

has no proper non-zero 2-ideals. Proof. This is an adaptation of a well-trodden argument, and relies on the fact that we work over a generic field. We use the notation L(b)0 to refer to the trivial representation in the 1-morphism category Hom Rep Ω 

> q

(b, b ) for any b ∈ Ω. Let I be a non-zero 2-ideal. Since Rep Ω 

> q

is a semisimple 2-category and I is a non-trivial 2-ideal, we have that I contains the identity 2-morphism id L of some object L in Hom Rep Ω 

> q

(a, a ′)for some a, a ′ ∈ Ω. Then L∗ ⊗ L in Hom Rep Ω 

> q

(a, a ) has the trivial representation L(a)0 as a direct summand, so that id L(a)0

is in I. For any b ∈ Ω, we then have id L̟ b−a ⊗L(a)0 ⊗L̟ a−b

is a 2-morphism in I. Since L(b)0 is a summand of L̟ b−a ⊗ L(a)0 ⊗ L̟ a−b , it follows that id L(b)0

is in I for any b ∈ Ω. For any object M in Hom Rep Ω 

> q

(a, a ′) for any a, a ′ ∈ Ω, it follows that QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 75 

id M = id M ⊗ id L(a)0

is in I. Containing the identity 2-morphisms of all 1-morphisms, one concludes that I contains all 2-morphisms as well. 

Lemma 5.26. The 2-functor GS ζ is faithful on 2-morphisms, i.e., the maps induced on the 2-morphism spaces are injective. Proof. Let ker( GS ζ ) denote the 2-ideal consisting of 2-morphisms f such that GS ζ (f ) = 0. Since GS ζ sends the identity 2-morphism of L̟ k ∈ Hom Rep Ω 

> q

(0 , k ) to the identity 2-morphism of the corresponding indecomposable singular Soergel bimodule Bψ0(̟ k ), we have that these identity 2-morphisms are not in ker( GS ζ ), so that ker( GS ζ ) is a proper ideal. Theorem 5.25 then implies that ker( GS ζ ) is trivial, so that GS ζ is faithful on 2-morphisms 15 . 

Recall the following notion of a degree zero equivalence .

Definition 5.28. [Eli17, Definition 2.14] A 2-functor G from a k-linear 2-category A to a 

k-linear graded 2-category B is a degree-zero equivalence if the following properties hold. (a) G induces a bijection between the objects. (b) G induces an isomorphism Hom A(M, N ) → Hom 0

> B

(G(M ), G(N )) to the space of mor-phisms of degree 0 (for 1-morphisms M, N in A with same source and target). (c) Hom k

> B

(G(M ), G(N )) = 0 for all k < 0 (for 1-morphisms M and N in A). (d) Every 1-morphism in B is isomorphic to a direct sum of grading shifts of 1-morphisms of the form G(M ) for 1-morphisms M in the Karoubi envelope of A; i.e. G is essentially surjective up to grading shift .The following theorem is then an immediate consequence of Theorem 5.23, Theorem 5.27 and the Williamson-Soergel categorification theorem.

Theorem 5.29. The 2-functors GS ζ : Webs Ω → mSSBim, GS ζ : Rep Ω

Remark 5.30. The definition of a degree zero equivalence, especially conditions (b) and (c), may remind one of the inclusion of the abelian heart of a t-structure into a (monoidal) dg-category (provided the monoidal structure restricts nicely to the heart). Here is an explicit geometric example relevant to us. Let O, K respectively denote the formal power series ring over C and its fraction field. Fix sheaf coefficients to be a field k of characteristic zero, and let Semis SL n(O)×SL n(O)(SL n(K)) denote the category of SL n(O) × SL n(O)-equivariant semisim-ple complexes of perverse sheaves on the loop group SL n(K), with Hom •(A, B ) given by ⊕ 

> i

Hom Db (A, B [i]) (where Db is the SL n(O) × SL n(O)-equivariant constructible derived cat-egory on the loop group). Then the inclusion of the perverse heart Perv SL n(O)×SL n(O)(SL n(K)) ֒ → Semis SL n(O)×SL n(O)(SL n(K)) (5.32) is a degree zero equivalence (considering both sides as monoidal categories with the convolution product). While there are no non-trivial extensions between perverse sheaves within the abelian heart, there may be non-vanishing positive degree morphisms between them in the equivariant derived category. From usual geometric Satake equivalence, the LHS of (5.32) is equivalent to Rep k(PGL n). The RHS of (5.32) may moreover be identified with SSBim( ̂0,̂ 0) constructed from the Kac-Moody realization, so that the inclusion Rep(PGL n)֒ → S SBim( ̂0,̂ 0) so obtained is the restriction of the (non-quantized, Soergelified) Geometric Satake 2-functor GS : Rep Ω →

mSSBim to the full subcategory with a single object 0 ∈ Ω. Summarizing the results from Theorem 5.17, Theorem 5.18, Theorem 5.20, Theorem 5.29, we have the following theorem.

I = H, and discussed some (but not all) of their mirror images and arrow reversals. This was enough to force the constraints on the scalars λ and ν which were needed for Theorem 5.20. Thanks to this lemma we do not need to check rotations, color changes via σ, or versions of relations obtained via Υ. For the remaining relations in Webs Ω, namely (6.11) and (6.15) below, we only check one version of the relation. However, we must also verify the mirror images (resp. arrow reversals) of all the relations in Webs Ω, and this does not correspond to a symmetry in D. Thankfully, the remaining mirror images and arrow reversals can be obtained alternatively from ones we have checked using only rotation and Υ. 6.5. Bursting a digon. 7.1. From extended affine to affine. Most, if not all, of the content in this subsection is well-known to experts, but this subsection is warranted by the lack of a consolidated reference in the literature (as far as the authors are aware). We retain the notation as in Subsection 2.7: 

W denotes the affine Weyl group, Wext = Ω ⋉ W denotes the extended affine Weyl group of PGL n, ω is a generator of Ω ⊂ Wext , with ωs iω−1 = si+1 , and ωn = 1, ̟ k denotes the fundamental weight for 1 ≤ k ≤ n − 1, sh : Λ wt → Ω denotes the quotient map with respect to the root lattice. While Wext is not a Coxeter group, the length function on W extends to a length function on Wext such that ℓ(ωk) = 0 for any k.Since Wext = Ŵ 0 ⋉ Λweight , double cosets in Ŵ 0\Wext /W ̂ 0 have a unique representative in Λweight /W ̂ 0 which is naturally in bijection with Λ +weight . We refer to elements of Ŵ 0\Wext /W ̂ 0

as spherical double cosets , and the double coset Ŵ 0tλŴ 0 as the spherical double coset of λ.Note that when λ ∈ Λ+root , the spherical double coset of λ is an element of Ŵ 0\W/W ̂ 0 ⊂

Ŵ 0\Wext /W ̂ 0.

This subsection is dedicated to explaining precisely how the diagrammatic symmetries of horizontal and vertical flipping (with suitable arrow reversals) connect to certain standard symmetries of 

SBS Bim (and hence SSBim). Nothing in this section should surprise the experts. While we only discuss this in the context of SBS Bim from the deformed affine Frealization, the contents of this section generalizes to any (partial, graded or ungraded) Frobenius hypercube Γ, with 

SBS Bim replaced by the algebraic category C(Γ) from [EWS16]. We retain notation from Subsection 5.3. 

Definition 7.17. Given a singular multistep expression 

IK • = [[ I0 ⊂ K1 ⊃ I1 ⊂ . . . ⊂ Kd ⊃ Id]] ,

we define the reverse of the multistep expression IK • to be R( IK •) := [[ Id ⊂ Kd ⊃ Id−1 ⊂ . . . ⊃ I1 ⊂ K1 ⊃ I0]] .

Definition 7.18. Let R denote the 2-functor on Dpre which fixes regions, flips diagrams horizontally (i.e., reflects diagrams across a vertical axis) and fixes floating polynomials. This 92 QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 

2-functor also necessarily changes some strand orientations to make the diagram consistent with the region labels. Note that for a 1-morphism in Dpre given by a singlestep expression I•,R( I•) is just the reverse of the singlestep expression I•.

Definition 7.19. Let D denote the 2-functor on Dpre which fixes regions and flips diagrams vertically (i.e., reflects diagrams across a horizontal axis) and fixes floating polynomials. This 2-functor also necessarily changes some strand orientations to make the diagram consistent with the region labels. Note that for a 1-morphism in Dpre given by a singlestep expression I•,D( I•) = I•.There are a few conventions in the literature for the corresponding functors on SBS Bim, we follow those in [BBE26, (3.26), (3.48)].

−→ Hom SBS Bim (J, I ) : I MJ 7 → J MI (ℓ(J) − ℓ(I)) .

RI , R J ) and adds a grading shift; R does not change the underlying functions of 2-morphisms. This functor is a weak 2-functor which is contravariant on 1-morphisms and covariant on 2-morphisms, and as such includes the coherence data given by the 2-morphisms R( RJ NRK ) ⊗RJ R( RI MRJ ) ∼

Remark 7.23. That this category is closed under R and D then follows from Theorem 7.26 and Theorem 7.25 below, or by direct checks on the monoidal generators. Definition 7.28. Let rot be the 2-functor on Dpre , given by rotating a diagram by 180 degrees. This is a strict 2-functor which coincides with the composition R ◦ D = D ◦ R. On SBS Bim, we define rot to be the (weak) 2-functor given by the composition D ◦ R. The following then easily follows from Theorem 7.27. 

Corollary 7.29. The 2-functor Φ : Dpre → S BS Bim intertwines rot with the 2-functor rot = D ◦ R (as well as with the 2-functor R ◦ D) on SBS Bim. 

Remark 7.30. In Dpre , the rotation 2-functor rot is more than just the composition of two flip symmetries; it is also realized by composition with caps and cups. That is, rot agrees with the 2-functor arising from the biadjunction of [ I ⊂ Ii ] and [ Ii ⊃ I]. After applying Φ one obtains the 2-functor arising from the bijadunction of induction and restriction, with units and counits of adjunction determined by the Frobenius extension structures. In particular, this corollary implies that biadjunction on SBS Bim agrees 18 coherently with the composition of two other symmetries D and R, which is not a priori obvious.

(M, R I )(2 ℓ(J) − 2ℓ(I)) (7.29) with suitable changes to the coherence 2-morphisms in (7.21) and (7.21). This 2-functor is related to D by the equality (not just an isomorphism) of 2-functors ˜D = R ◦ D ◦ R. (7.30)

Remark 7.33. One can similarly also define a left (global) intersection form ˜〈− , −〉 IK • on BS( IK •), which will be a symmetric ( RI0 , R Id )-invariant non-degenerate (left) RI0 -bilinear form valued in RI0 , using the self duality isomorphisms (7.31) for the left dual ˜D in Theo-rem 7.31. Remark 7.34. In Williamson’s thesis [Wil08] where singular Soergel bimodules are originally defined, it is shown that the functor of tensoring with an induction or restriction bimodule commutes with duality, and the natural isomorphism involved is defined using the Frobenius structure, precisely as ηI• for the cases when I• equals [ I ⊂ Ii ] or [ Ii ⊃ I]. This happens in [Wil08, §3.2] and [Wil08, Proposition 4.3.4], though the details are omitted from the published QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 97 

article [Wil11]. Iterating this natural isomorphism over iterated tensor products, one obtains 

ηI• for any singlestep expression I•. This approach is also taken in [BBE26]. Meanwhile, the subsequent literature tended to define the isomorphism between a Bott-Samelson bimodule and its dual using the global intersection form. References include [EW14] and [EMTW20] for ordinary Soergel bimodules, and [Pat21, Appendix A] for one-sided-singular Soergel bimodules, though we are unaware of a reference which treats global intersection forms for singular Soergel bimodules in general. These papers define the intersection form inductively, having chosen a particular top-degree element of each induction bimodule. In [Pat21, Appendix A], the Frobenius structure is explicitly used to choose a top-degree element. 

Remark 7.35. While we explicitly defined the 2-functors D and R only on SBS Bim, it is straightforward to extend these 2-functors, as well as the results involving them, to the Karoubi envelope SSBim. 7.4. Light ladders and light leaves: an example. As we illustrate a particular example, we briefly recall the purpose of elementary light leaves and ladders. We consider representations of sl n, and let Vk := Λ k(Cn). Let μ be a weight in Vk for some 

k. There is a minimal dominant weight λstart such that λend := λstart + μ is also dominant. If 

Lλ denotes the simple representation of highest weight λ, then Lλend is a direct summand of 

Lλstart ⊗ Vk, and we are interested in constructing the projection map. Now Lλstart is a summand of a tensor product Vi1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ Vid of fundamental representations, when ∑dj=1 ̟ ij = λstart . Similarly, Lλend is a summand of Vj1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ Vje . The elementary light ladder ELL( μ) is a particular morphism ELL( μ) : ( Vi1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ Vid ) ⊗ Vk → (Vj1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ Vje )which remains nonzero after pre- and post-composition with the appropriate idempotents, so that it descends to a nonzero (projection) map 

Lλstart ⊗ Vk → Lλend .

Remark 7.36. If λ is any dominant weight such that λ + μ is dominant, then λ − λstart is also dominant. The projection map Lλ ⊗ Vk → Lλ+μ can be obtained from id ⊗ ELL( μ) by pre- and post-composition with the appropriate idempotents. Here id represents the identity map of a tensor product of fundamental representations associated with λ − λstart .Here is a particular example when n ≥ 5 and k = 2. Let μ =̟ 4 −̟ 3 +̟ 2 −̟ 1. Then 

λstart =̟ 1 +̟ 3, and λend =̟ 2 +̟ 4. Note that Lλstart is a summand of V1 ⊗ V3 and Lλend 

is a summand of V2 ⊗ V4. Then ELL( μ) : ( V1 ⊗ V3) ⊗ V2 → (V2 ⊗ V4), ELL( μ) = 0

. (7.55) Now we wish to compose ELL([ qend ⊃ p]) with ELL([ p ⊂ qstart ]) ⊗ id [+2] to obtain a mor-phism comparable with GS(ELL( μ)) from (7.39). However, the sources and targets of these morphisms do not match, because they use different reduced expressions for qstart and qend and 

p. These different reduced expressions were essential aspects of the algorithm, since at various stages we were required to use reduced expressions starting with zstart or zend . Different reduced expressions for the same double coset are related by the (singular) braid relations, introduced in [EK23], and to each braid relation we have a corresponding diagrammatic morphism called a rex move, introduced in [EKLP24, §6]. To compose these elementary light leaves we should insert rex moves in between. Examples of rex moves are: crossings of upward-oriented strands (the up-up move), crossings of downward oriented strands (down-down), sideways crossings where the colors commute in the ambient parabolic subgroup (commuting switchback), and the type A switchback maps which look like a cup or cap straddling two other strands, see [EKLP24, Example 6.1]. See also Theorem 7.37. QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE 101

Remark 7.37. Let D flip diagrams upside-down and reverse orientations without changing region labels. The composition rex 3 ◦D(rex 3) is equal to the identity map. This is called the 

switchback relation and is proven in [EKLP24, Theorem 6.7] as a consequence of the relations from [EWS16]. A prototypical depiction of this relation is [BBE26, Lemma 3.4]. 102 QUANTUM SATAKE IN TYPE A: THE GENERAL AND GENERIC CASE

(7.59) From here, one can use a series of the relations: oriented R3 (5.19), distant R3 (5.21), distant R2 (5.18), and switchback (see Theorem 7.37), eventually deducing that the diagrams in (7.59) and (7.39) are equal. We leave this exercise to the reader. We note that the colors 1 , 3 ∈ Ω (navy blue and orange) do not label strands in (7.39).
